14 - 变分自编码器章节介绍

大家好，欢迎回到《无监督深度学习》第二部分。

这节课，我们会讨论一些变分自编码器背后的背景知识。

变分自编码器是一种神经网络，它既能学会复现自己的输入，也能将训练数据映射到一个隐空间，然后通过从这个隐空间采样，来从数据分布中抽取样本。

如果这听起来有点复杂，别担心，在接下来的几节课里，我们会更详细地走完这个过程。

首先，我们来稍微聊一下“变分自编码器”这个名字。

如果你一直在跟我的课程，那你很可能认得其中一个术语，但不认识另一个。

那么，我们先从简单的那个说起。

我们知道什么是自编码器。

实际上，作为这门课的热身，我们还动手实践搭建了一个。

自编码器就是一种能够学习去复现其输入的神经网络。

自编码器的一个重要特征是，我们通常会将隐藏单元的数量选得比输入维度小。

这就制造了一个瓶颈，迫使神经网络去学习数据的一种更紧凑的表示。

那么，这到底是什么意思呢？

嗯，假设我们能教会这个自编码器忠实地复现输入。

再假设这个自编码器的输入尺寸是七百八十四个维度，而隐藏层大小是二百个维度。

这就意味着，我们学会了用少得多的编码，也就是二百个数，来表征我们传进去的任何图像，而原本的图像是由七百八十四个数构成的。

那么，这又意味着什么呢？

这意味着，在原先那七百八十四个数里，有很多是冗余的。

原始数据中包含的真实信息量，肯定要远少于七百八十四。

通常，我们把隐藏层里的这二百个数称为隐变量表示，而且我们一般用字母 z 来表示它。

这应该不会让你太意外，因为我们在过去一直用字母 z 来表示神经网络的隐藏层和隐变量。

我们稍后还会再触及这个概念。

变分自编码器里的另一个术语，也就是你可能没听说过的那个，是“变分”。

它指的是变分推断，或者说变分贝叶斯。

这些技术属于贝叶斯机器学习的范畴，通常你得修过一门级别较高的研究生机器学习课程，才能学到这些。

一个理解变分推断的方式是，把它看作是期望最大化，也就是 EM 算法的扩展。

事先提个醒，这里讲的许多信息，跟变分自编码器具体如何工作并没有直接联系。

这些只是某种历史背景，为的是让你对整体脉络有更好的把握。

所以，如果你没太弄明白，也别担心，要理解变分自编码器的底层机制，并不需要掌握这些。

你或许还记得，当我们手头有一个隐变量模型，却又无法直接最大化 x 的概率 p 时，就会用到期望最大化算法。

高斯混合模型就是这个情况的一个例子，我们之前已经见过了。

期望最大化算法给出的是参数的点估计。

换言之，它可以被看作是一种频率学派统计方法。

而变分推断所做的，就是把这个想法扩展到贝叶斯领域，在那里我们学的不是参数的点估计，而是它们的分布。

那么，这是不是意味着，要理解这门课你就得先去弄懂变分推断呢？

幸好，并非如此。

你听了一定很开心，总算有一个话题，不是本课程的先修要求了。

那么，为什么我们不需要理解变分推断，也能弄懂变分自编码器呢？

嗯，拥有更深的理解当然有助于掌握变分自编码器，但要学习它的工作原理并用代码实现它，并不需要这种深层的理解。

而且正如我之前提到的，如果哪天你真的决定要去学习变分推断以及类似的贝叶斯方法，请意识到那会极具挑战性，在概念上和数学上都会非常难，所以，把它设为这门课的先修条件也是不切实际的。

那么，以上就是关于哪些元素结合起来造就了变分自编码器的一些背景。

它将一件我们了解的东西，自编码器，和一件我们很可能完全不了解的东西，变分推断，结合在了一起。

在接下来的几节课里，我们来看看训练变分自编码器并从中采样的具体机制，以及它的代码实现。

英文原文

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class, Unsupervised Deep Learning Part 2.

In this lecture, we are going to discuss some of the background behind the variational autoencoder.

The variational autoencoder is a neural network that can both learn to reproduce its input, but also map training data to a latent space and then draw samples from the data distribution by sampling from the latent space.

If that sounds complicated, don't worry as we will be going through that process in much more detail throughout the next few lectures.

First, let's discuss the name a little bit, variational autoencoder.

If you've been following my courses, then you probably recognize one of these terms, but not the other.

So let's discuss the easy one first.

We know what an autoencoder is.

In fact, we practice building one as a warm-up to this course.

An autoencoder is just a neural network that learns to reproduce its input.

One important feature of autoencoders is that typically we choose the number of hidden units to be smaller than the input dimensionality.

This creates a bottleneck and forces the neural network to learn a more compact representation of the data.

So what do we mean by that?

Well, suppose we can teach this autoencoder to faithfully reproduce its input.

Let's assume that the input size to this autoencoder is 784 dimensions and the hidden layer size is 200 dimensions.

That means we have learned to represent whatever image we've passed in, which consists of 784 numbers as a much smaller code instead, 200 numbers.

Well, what does this mean?

That means out of the original 784 numbers, many of them were redundant.

The true amount of information contained in the original data must then be much less than 784.

We typically call these 200 numbers in the hidden layer the latent variable representation, and we typically use the letter Z to represent it.

This shouldn't come as a surprise since we've consistently used the letter Z to represent the hidden layers of the neural network and latent variables in the past.

We'll touch on this concept again later.

The other term in variational autoencoder, which you probably haven't heard of, is variational.

This refers to variational inference or variational Bayes.

These techniques fall into the realm of Bayesian machine learning, which you'd have to have taken a difficult graduate course in machine learning to learn about.

One way to think of variational inference is that it's an extension of the expectation maximization or EM algorithm.

Just a warning, a lot of this information is not directly related to how variational autoencoders work.

It's just sort of historical information to give you a better sense of the big picture.

So if you don't quite get it, don't worry, it's not required to understand the underlying mechanics of the variational autoencoder.

As you may recall, the expectation maximization algorithm is used when we have a latent variable model and we can't maximize p of x directly.

An example of this is the Gaussian mixture model, which we've seen already.

The expectation maximization algorithm gives us a point estimate of the parameters.

In other words, it can be seen as a frequentist statistical method.

What variational inference does is it extends this idea to the Bayesian realm, where instead of learning point estimates of the parameters, we learn their distributions instead.

So does this mean you have to understand variational inference in order to understand this course?

Luckily, no.

You must be happy to hear that there is finally one topic that is not a prerequisite to this course.

So why don't we need to understand variational inference in order to understand variational autoencoders?

Well, it would certainly help to have a deeper understanding of variational autoencoders, but learning how one works mechanically and implementing one in code do not require this deep understanding.

And as I mentioned earlier, if you do decide one day that you want to learn about variational inference and similar Bayesian methods, realize that it will be extremely challenging, very difficult conceptually and mathematically,so it wouldn't be practical to make it a prerequisite to this course.

So that's just some background on what elements combine to give us variational autoencoders.

It takes one thing we know about, autoencoders, and one thing we probably don't know anything about, variational inference, and combines them together.

In the next few lectures, we'll look at the mechanics of training and sampling from a variational autoencoder and an implementation in code.

15 - 变分自编码器架构

大家好，欢迎回到《无监督深度学习》第二部分。

这节课，我们来讨论一下变分自编码器背后的架构设计。

那么，如你所知，变分自编码器是一种神经网络。

它由两部分构成：编码器和解码器。

它们在中间分割开来，而正如我们讨论过的，这个中间层的尺寸通常比输入要小。

我们通常把在这个特定隐藏层上的值称为 z，它们代表着输入数据的隐变量表示。

我们来谈谈数据是如何在这个神经网络里流动的。

这里的一个关键点在于，它的运作方式与传统的自编码器不一样。

记住，传统自编码器的运行方式和前馈神经网络完全相同。

在每一层，你拿输入乘以权重，加上偏置，然后应用激活函数。

但在变分自编码器中，在编码器的末端会发生一些奇怪的事情。

具体来说，我们得到的不是一个数值，而是一个分布，或者更精确地说，我们得到的是某个分布的参数。

记住，贝叶斯机器学习，全是关于学习“分布”，而不是学习“点估计”。

因此，我们不是真的去找到 z，而是去找到 z 的分布 q，一个能告诉我们 z 的概率密度函数是怎样的分布。

至于如何计算这些参数的具体细节，我们稍后会深入探讨，但现在，我们只需假设，我们能通过某种方式，把编码器的输出转变成一个均值和方差，这样 z 的分布 q 就只是一个带着那个均值和方差的高斯分布。

那么，以上就是变分自编码器的前半部分。

我们抵达了编码器的末端，现在必须经过解码器。

此时，我们手头有一个 z 的分布 q。

我们需要从这个分布里拿到真实的数值，才能输入到神经网络的剩余部分中去。

所以，我们当然是要从 z 的分布 q 里抽取一个样本。

现在，我们有了一个样本 z 向量，可以把它像平常一样输入解码器：乘以权重，加上偏置，应用激活函数，以此类推。

最终，我们到达解码器的输出端。

在这里我们又要做什么呢？

嗯，这并不让人意外，这里的输出同样也是一个分布。

我们假设输入是一个二值变量，那么我们的输出也会是一个二值变量。

换句话说，它们只能取零和一这两个值。

这当然就是伯努利分布，它由一个参数来表示，这个参数告诉了我们得到一的概率是多少。

如你所知，sigmoid 函数能给出一个介于零和一之间的值，因此，sigmoid 是个合适的激活函数，这样解码器的输出就能用来表征伯努利分布了。

实际上，这和我们做二分类时的做法差别不大。

我们都知道，做二分类时，我们会用 sigmoid，其输出就代表了给定 x 下 y 等于一的概率 p。

然而，我们通常把它视为一个“预测值”，而不是一个概率分布。

换句话说，我们对它所做的，仅仅是四舍五入来得到预测的标签。

但在这里，我们要明确地将两者区分开来。

既然我们试图更遵循贝叶斯方法，那解码器的输出就代表着一个概率分布。

要记住的一件事是，正因为输出是一个分布，这会影响到我们如何使用它。

在做二分类的常规神经网络里，没错，我们确有一个给定 x 下 y 等于一的概率 p，但我们只是把它四舍五入来得到预测。

而在变分自编码器中，范式不同了。

在解码器的输出端，我们得到的是一个分布。

我们要从这个分布里，去生成样本。

好了，这节课的内容全都是关于如何在变分自编码器中完成前向传播。

总结一下，一个变分自编码器由两部分组成：编码器和解码器。

编码器的末端是一个瓶颈，也就是说，它的维度通常比输入要小。

它是一个紧凑的编码，作用上像是输入的一个压缩版本。

我们称此处的值为 z，但编码器输出实际给我们的，是 z 的均值和方差。

换句话说，我们实际上并不是用编码器来计算 z 的，而是用它来计算 z 的分布，也就是 z 的分布 q。

接下来是解码器。

为了把我们的输入送入解码器，我们从 z 的分布 q 中抽取一个样本，而正如我们讨论过的，这是一个高斯分布。

然后，我们照常使这个样本通过神经网络解码器。

到这里，我们将把输入和输出都视为二值变量，这意味着输出分布将是伯努利分布，我们可以用 sigmoid 函数来得到它的参数。

顺便说一下，正因为输出实际代表的是一个分布，我们最终对它的用途会是去生成样本。

它并不像二分类那样，仅仅是把输出做个四舍五入就完事了。

英文原文

Hey everyone, and welcome back to this class, Unsupervised Deep Learning Part 2.

In this lecture, we are going to discuss the architecture behind the variational autoencoder.

So, as you know, the variational autoencoder is a neural network.

It comes in two parts, the encoder and the decoder.

They're split in the middle, which as we discussed, is typically smaller than the input size.

We typically call the values at this particular hidden layer z, and they represent the latent variable representation of the input data.

Let's talk about how data flows through this neural network.

One key point here is that it does not work like a traditional autoencoder.

Remember that a traditional autoencoder works just like a feed-forward neural network.

At each layer, you multiply by the weight, add the bias, and apply the activation function.

With variational autoencoders, something strange happens at the end of the encoder.

In particular, we don't get a value, but we get a distribution, or more precisely, we get the parameters of a distribution.

Remember, Bayesian machine learning is all about learning distributions, not learning point estimates.

So, instead of actually finding z, we are finding q of z, a distribution that tells us the PDF of z.

We'll get into the specifics of just how to calculate those parameters later, but for now, let's just say that somehow we can turn the output of the encoder into a mean invariance, and then q of z is just a Gaussian with that mean and variance.

So, that's the first half of the variational autoencoder.

We get to the end of the encoder, now we have to go through the decoder.

So, we have a distribution q of z.

From this, we need actual numbers to pass in through the rest of the neural network.

So, of course, what we do is draw a sample from Q of Z.

Now that we have a sample Z vector, we can pass it through the decoder as usual, multiply by the weight, add the bias, apply the activation function, and so on.

Finally, we get to the output of the decoder.

What do we do here?

Well, not surprisingly, this is also a distribution.

Let's assume our input is a binary variable, so that our output is also a binary variable.

In other words, they only take on the values 0 and 1.

Well this is of course the Bernoulli distribution, and it is represented by one parameter which tells us the probability of getting a one.

As you know, a sigmoid gives us a value in between zero and one, and therefore a sigmoid is the appropriate activation function so that the output of the decoder can represent Bernoulli distributions.

This is actually not much different than what we do for binary classification.

We know that for binary classification, we use a sigmoid and the output represents p of y equals 1 given x.

However, we typically think of that as a prediction rather than a probability distribution.

In other words, all we do with it is round it to get the predicted label.

Here we are explicitly making that distinction.

Since we want to try to be more Bayesian, the output of the decoder represents a probability distribution.

One thing to keep in mind is that because the output is a distribution, that's going to affect how we use it.

With regular neural networks doing binary classification, yes, we have a p of y equals one given x, but we just round it to get a prediction.

With variational autoencoders, the paradigm is different.

At the output of the decoder, we have a distribution.

From this distribution, we will then generate samples.

Okay, so this lecture was all about how to do the forward pass in a variational autoencoder.

To summarize, a variational autoencoder is made up of two parts the encoder and the decoder.

The end of the encoder is a bottleneck, meaning its dimensionality is typically smaller than the input.

It is a compact code that acts like a compressed version of the input.

We call the value here z, but what the output of the encoder actually gives us is the mean invariance of z.

In other words, we're not actually using the encoder to calculate z, but rather to calculate the distribution of z, q of z.

Next, we have the decoder.

To get our input into the decoder, we draw a sample from q of z, which as we've discussed is a Gaussian.

Then we pass it through the neural network decoder as normal.

Now at this point, we are going to treat the inputs and outputs as binary variables, which means the output distribution is going to be Bernoulli, which we can use the sigmoid to get the parameters for.

And by the way, since the output actually represents a distribution, our eventual use for it will be to generate samples.

It's not like binary classification where we just round the output.

16 - 用神经网络参数化高斯分布

大家好，欢迎回到《无监督深度学习》第二部分。

在上一节课，我们谈到了一个想法，那就是编码器网络的输出是一个分布，而不是一个单一的值。

这让很多人感到困惑，所以这节课专门就是为了理清这个困惑。

我们还会写一些代码来演示这个想法。

顺便提一句，如果你上过我那门主要讲强化学习的高级AI课程，你应该已经见过一个类似的例子了。

当然，要理解这门课，那并非必要，但会有所帮助。

那么，我们来假设我们有一个代表编码器的神经网络，它的各层尺寸分别是四、三、二。

这意味着输入数据的维度是四，隐藏层的大小是三，而隐向量z的大小是二。

这表示在这个网络的末端，我们要去表征一个二维的高斯分布。

注意，为简单起见，我们会表征一个轴对齐的高斯分布，而不是完整的协方差高斯，那种需要更多的参数。

换句话说，我们用一个向量，而不是矩阵，就能表示方差，每个维度上一个元素。

那我们要怎么做呢？

嗯，关键是得让最后一层的尺寸，变成所指定大小的两倍。

所以，如果我们想表征一个二维高斯，最后一层的尺寸就需要是四。

前两个分量将代表均值的各个分量，后两个分量则代表标准差的各个分量。

好的，这很简单，但还有另一个问题。

我们知道标准差必须是一个严格的正数，但神经网络却可以输出任何数字，不论正负。

那我们怎么解决这个问题呢？

一个简单的办法是使用softplus激活函数。

softplus就像是一个平滑版的relu。

当输入很大时，它近似于线性增长；当输入很小时，它看起来更像指数函数。

最重要的是，它既平滑又连续，这意味着它是可微的，并且其值永远大于零。

那么，我们现在来看看代码。

课程仓库中的相关文件是 parametrize_gaussian.py。

文件开头是一些标准导入，numpy、matplotlib，还有来自scipy的多元正态分布。

接下来，我们定义了softplus函数。

请注意，我们是怎样调用了一个特殊的numpy函数，叫做log1p，它会自动给传入的参数加上一。

这样做的原因是因为它在数值上更稳定。

接着，我们定义了神经网络的权重，这只是一个单隐藏层的神经网络。

输入大小是四，隐藏层大小是三，而输出大小是二。

我们想让输出大小是二，这样就能把样本绘制在散点图上。

请注意，第一个权重的尺寸是四乘三，但第二个权重的尺寸是三乘四，而不是三乘二。

这是因为我需要两个输出来表征输出分布的均值，另外两个输出来表征它的标准差。

接下来是前向传播函数，它采用了标准的前馈神经网络传递方式，并使用tanh激活函数。

请注意，在末尾，我们是怎样将最终的输出拆分开来，让前一半代表均值，而后一半代表标准差的。

然后我们返回均值和标准差。

接着，我们随机生成一个尺寸为四的输入向量，并把它传入神经网络，以获取均值和标准差。

请记住，这多少代表着给定x下z的模型分布q。

接下来，我们用这个均值和标准差，从一个高斯分布中抽取一万个样本。

请注意，多变量正态模块接收的参数是协方差，它在对角线上是方差，因此我们需要对标准差进行平方。

最后，拿到这些样本后，我们把它们绘制在散点图上。

你可以用这些数据的可视化结果，来确认它确实具备由这个神经网络计算出的均值和标准差。

那么，我们运行一下看看结果。

好，我们计算出的均值大约在负二点四和负一点九的位置。

标准差分别是二点二和二点一六，而这些样本看起来确实是从这个分布中抽取出来的。

嗯，虽然这段代码相当简单，但它在概念上却和我们通常做的事情不大一样。

那么，我们来逐项拆解一下各个组成部分。

第一，我们在神经网络中做一次前向传播。

这属于常规操作。

第二，神经网络的输出不是一个值，而是一个分布。

这就有所不同了。

第三，我们可以从这个分布中生成样本，而这正是我们在变分自编码器里要做的。

第四点，我们还没有谈到，要如何去训练这些权重。

事实上，有人可能会好奇，当我们最终得到的是一个来自分布的样本，而非一个关于我们想要更新参数的函数值时，我们该如何进行梯度下降。

这是个值得思考的问题。

一个关键点是，这个来自编码器的输出分布，它是输入的函数。

我们称它为z的分布q，但它对x的依赖关系并不明显。

当然，我们也可以直接称它为给定x下z的分布q，让这种依赖变得很明确。

为了让事情更清晰，了解一下这个z的分布q到底是在尝试近似什么，会很有用。

具体来说，它是在尝试近似给定x下z的分布p，也就是一个依赖于x是什么的、关于z的分布。

我们可以将给定x下z的分布p，视为给定x下z的真实条件分布，而将给定x下z的分布q，视为我们的神经网络对分布p的近似。

等稍后开始讨论代价函数以及如何优化它的时候，你就会知道了解这一点很有用了。

英文原文

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class, Unsupervised Deep Learning, Part 2.

In the previous lecture, we talked about the idea that the output of the encoder network is going to be a distribution rather than a single value.

This is confusing for a lot of people, so this lecture is designed to clear up that confusion.

We're also going to write some code to demonstrate this idea.

And by the way, if you took my advanced AI course, which focuses on reinforcement learning, you would have already seen a similar example.

Of course, that won't be necessary to understand this course, but it would help.

So let's say we have a neural network representing the encoder with the layer sizes four, three, and two.

This means that the input data is of dimensionality four, the hidden layer size is three, and the size of a latent vector z is two.

This means at the end of this, we are going to represent a two-dimensional Gaussian.

Note that for simplicity, we will be representing an axis aligned Gaussian rather than a full covariance Gaussian, which would require even more parameters.

In other words, we can represent the variance with a single vector instead of a matrix with one element per dimension.

So how can we do this?

Well, the key is to actually make the final layer size double what it is specified as.

So if we want to represent a 2D Gaussian, you'll need a final layer size of 4.

The first two components will represent the components of the mean, and the second two components will represent the components of the standard deviation.

Okay, so that's pretty simple, but there is one more problem.

We know that the standard deviation must be a strictly positive number, but a neural network can output any number, positive or negative.

So how can we fix this?

A simple solution is to use the soft plus activation function.

The soft plus is like a smooth version of the rel U.

It increases approximately linearly when the input is large and looks more like an exponential when the input is small.

Most importantly, it's smooth and continuous, which means it's differentiable and it's always bigger than zero.

So now let's take a look at the code.

The relevant file in the course repo is parameterize Gaussian dot py.

At the top we have some standard imports, numpy, matplotlib, and the multivariate normal from scipy.

Next, we define the soft plus function.

Note how we are using a special numpy function called log1p, which automatically adds one to the argument it's passed in.

The reason for this is that it is more numerically stable.

Next, we define the weights of our neural network, which is just a one hidden layer neural net.

The input size is four, the hidden size is three, and the output size is two.

We want the output size to be two so that we can plot the samples in a scatterplot.

Notice how the first weight is of size four by three, but the second weight is of size three by four instead of three by two.

This is because we need two outputs to represent the mean of the output distribution and two outputs to represent the standard deviation of the output distribution.

Next, we have the forward function, which does a standard feed forward pass through the neural network with a tanh activation function.

Notice how at the end, we split up the final outputs so that the first half represents the mean and the second half represents the standard deviation.

We then return the mean and standard deviation.

Next, we create a random input vector of size 4, and we pass this through the neural network to get the mean and standard deviation.

Remember, this represents sort of a model distribution Q of z given x.

Next, we draw 10,000 samples from a Gaussian with this mean and standard deviation.

Note that the MVN module takes in a covariance, which has variances along the diagonal, so we need to square the standard deviation.

Finally, once we have the samples, we plot them on a scatterplot.

You can use this visual representation of the data to confirm that it has the mean and standard deviation calculated by the neural network.

So let's run this and see what we get.

Okay, so the mean we calculated is at minus 2.4 and minus 1.9.

The standard deviation is 2.2 and 2.16 and these samples seem to have been drawn from this distribution.

Now while this code was pretty simple, it is conceptually different from what we usually do.

So let's break down the components.

Number one, we're doing a forward pass through a neural network.

This is business as usual.

Number two, the output of the neural network is not a value but a distribution.

This is different.

Number three, from this distribution, we can generate samples, which is what we'll be doing in the variational autoencoder.

And number four, we haven't yet talked about how we're going to train these weights.

In fact, one might wonder how we can do gradient descent when what we end up with is a sample from a distribution rather than a value that is a function of the parameters we want to update.

This is a question worth thinking about.

One key point is that this output distribution from the encoder is a function of the input.

We call it Q of Z, but the dependence on X isn't clear.

Of course, we can just call it Q of Z given X to make the dependency explicit.

To make things more clear, it's useful to know what this Q of Z is trying to approximate.

In particular, it's trying to approximate P of Z given X, a distribution over Z that depends on what X is.

We can think of P of Z given X as the true conditional distribution of Z given X, and Q of Z given X as our neural network's approximation of P.

This will be useful to know later when we start discussing the class function and how to optimize it.

17 - 隐空间、预测分布与样本生成

大家好，欢迎回到《无监督深度学习》第二部分。

有一个既适用于变分自编码器，也适用于普通自编码器的重要概念，就是隐空间这个概念。

请记住，我们的神经网络由两步构成：编码器和解码器。

编码器负责将输入数据 x 转换成另一个向量 z，也就是 x 的一种编码版本。

这个 x 的编码版本，就存在于隐空间中。

然后，取这个隐空间中的一个向量，让它通过解码器，我们就能得回一张跟原始图像相似的图片。

试图将这个过程可视化是很有用的，因为它能帮我们理解由此能做的各种不同操作。

那么，我们的自编码器最终会学会做什么呢？

嗯，它将会占据隐空间中的一块区域，并对其进行本质上的划分。

为简单起见，假设我们用的是 MNIST 数据集。

我们有从零到九的数字。

这意味着这十个数字，一定会存在于这个区域中的某个地方。

那么，这个自编码器在学的是什么呢？

它在学习如何拿一张图像，找到它在这块区域中属于哪个位置。

它同时也在学习，如何从这块区域中数字所在的位置，取出任何一个数字，再把它变回那个数字的图像。

这块区域就像是一个微小的空间，它能够表征图像，但占用的空间却只是完整图像的一小部分。

你可以把这想象成是一次压缩再解压的操作。

要压缩，我把图像转换成某种编码。

要解压，我把编码再转换回图像。

关键在于它是压缩过的，也就是说它更小。

所以，从某种意义上讲，变分自编码器是把所有训练数据都打包进了一个小空间，等以后你需要样本时，再把它解包拿出来。

那么，这为什么很重要呢？

这里要引入另一个借自贝叶斯机器学习的概念。

这同样是那种，即使你没有任何背景知识也能理解的东西。

但如果你刚好有贝叶斯机器学习的背景，那你接触这些概念时会觉得更得心应手。

这些概念就是：后验预测分布和先验预测分布。

而有了分布，我们就能生成样本，因此我们也就有了后验预测样本和先验预测样本。

现在，与其给出这些概念的方程式，因为如果你没有深入学过贝叶斯机器学习，这些式子会让人困惑而且也没什么用，我们不如通过示意图来审视这两个概念。

当然，如果你碰巧知道后验预测分布和先验预测分布是什么，那你就可以把你所知道的，和我们现在正在做的事情联系起来。

那么，对于后验预测样本，这个过程和我们之前描述过的、用于获得重建输入的前向机制完全一致。

我们传入输入，比方说是一张数字八的图像。

这张图会被映射到一个高斯分布，也就是给定 x 下 z 的分布 q 上。

然后，我们从这个分布里抽取一个样本 z，让它通过解码器，从而得到该图像重建版本的分布。

接着，我们可以从这个输出分布中采样出一张图像。

我们称其为后验预测样本。

现在，还有另一件我们能做的事。

我们不再从输入 x 获取 z，而是可以从标准正态分布中采样 z。

在一维情况下，这就是 N(零, 一)；在多维情况下，就是 N(零, 单位矩阵)。

至于为什么标准正态分布尤其有意义，你稍后就会明白。

一旦我们拿到了这个 z，就可以照常让它通过解码器。

通过这么做，我们可以得到一张看起来像是来自训练数据的图像，比如说，一张数字八的图像。

我们称这样的样本为先验预测样本。

当然，正因为我们只是从标准正态分布中采样，我们事先并不知道会得到哪个数字。

那么，这为什么行得通呢？

有意思的地方在于，考虑到 z 仅仅采样自标准正态分布，而非从某张输入图像推导得来，我们竟然能得到任何有意义的图像。

关键在于，我们将以一种特定方式来构建我们的优化算法，从而鼓励编码器把训练数据都映射到标准正态分布的周围。

如此一来，当我们从标准正态分布中采样时，所得的结果就应该能代表训练数据中的某个东西。

英文原文

Hey everyone, and welcome back to this class, Unsupervised Deep Learning Part 2.

One important concept that applies both to variational autoencoders and regular autoencoders is this idea of the latent space.

Remember, our neural network is made up of two steps, the encoder and the decoder.

The encoder is responsible for turning the input data x into a different vector z, a coded version of x.

This coded version of x lives in the latent space.

Then, taking a vector in this latent space, we can pass it through the decoder and get back an image that looks like the original.

What is useful is trying to visualize this process as it leads to the different operations we can do.

So, what is our autoencoder eventually going to learn how to do?

Well, it's going to take a blob of this latent space and essentially partition it.

So pretend for simplicity we're using MNIST.

We have the digits zero to nine.

That means these ten digits, they have to live somewhere on this blob.

So, what is the autoencoder learning how to do?

It's learning how to take an image and find out where on this blob it belongs.

It's also learning how to take any digit from where it lives on this blob and turn it back into an image of that digit.

The blob is like a tiny space that represents images, but takes up only a fraction of the space as a full image.

You can think of this as a compression decompression operation.

To compress, I transform the image into some code.

To decompress, I convert the code back into an image.

The key is that it's compressed, meaning it's smaller.

So the variational autoencoder is in some sense packing in all the training data into a small space and then unpacking it later when you request a sample.

Now, why is this important?

Here is another idea borrowed from Bayesian machine learning.

This is again one of those things that you don't need any background in order to understand.

But if you do happen to have a background in Bayesian machine learning, you will be more comfortable with these concepts.

These are the posterior predictive distribution and the prior predictive distribution.

And from a distribution, we can generate samples, so we have the posterior predictive sample and the prior predictive sample.

Now rather than giving you equations for this, which can be confusing and are kind of useless if you haven't studied Bayesian machine learning in depth, we are going to look at these two concepts via diagrams.

Of course, if you do happen to know what the posterior predictive and prior predictive distributions are, you can relate what you know to what we're doing here.

So for the posterior predictive sample, the process is exactly what we described previously as the forward mechanism to get the reconstructed input.

We pass in the input, let's say it's an image of an eight.

This gets mapped to a Gaussian distribution Q of Z given X.

Then we take a sample Z from this distribution, pass it through the decoder to get a distribution for the reconstructed version of the image.

We can then sample an image from this output distribution.

We call this the posterior predictive sample.

Now here's something else we can do.

Instead of getting z from the input x, we can sample z from a standard normal.

That's n zero one in one dimension or n zero identity matrix in multiple dimensions.

You'll see why the standard normal is particularly meaningful later.

Once we get this C, we can pass it through the decoder as usual.

By doing this, we can obtain an image that looks like it comes from the training data, say an image of an eight.

We call this sample a prior predictive sample.

Of course, because we're just sampling from the standard normal, we don't know which digit we're going to get.

So why does this work?

What's interesting about it is we can get any meaningful image at all, considering z is just sampled from the standard normal rather than being derived from an input image.

The key is we're going to build our optimization algorithm in such a way that we'll encourage the encoder to map the training data around the standard normal.

And so when we sample from the standard normal, it should represent something from the training data.

18 - 损失函数

大家好，欢迎回到《无监督深度学习》第二部分。

这节课，我们要开始讨论如何训练一个自编码器。

记住，对于所有机器学习问题，我们通常都有两种运行模式。

一个是推断，另一个是训练或者说学习。

“推断”是一个宽泛且可能被过度使用的术语，但在深度学习的语境下，它通常指代前向过程，换言之，在监督学习里就是进行预测，而在无监督学习中，则是将数据转换为其隐式表征。

在 scikit-learn 里，这些通常由 fit 和 predict，或 fit 和 transform 这类函数来完成。

无论如何，下一步都是要去讨论拟合或者说训练。

像往常一样，我们想做的是定义一个代价函数，然后将其最小化。

所以，当我们谈到变分自编码器时，事情就在这里变得有点棘手了。

它的代价函数看起来会非常奇怪和陌生。

事实上，这是贯穿本课程的一个共同主题。

这部分内容，要是你了解变分推断，确实会对你大有裨益，但我们会通过从第一性原理，也就是你目前已经掌握的知识出发来进行探讨，这样也能把它讲通。

所以，不需要任何变分推断的背景知识。

我们先来看看代价函数是什么。

一旦看过了，我们再去问，它说得通吗？

或者换种问法，当自编码器在自己该做的事上表现越来越好时，这个函数的值会随之下降吗？

那么，这就是实际的视角。

随着自编码器在重建输入方面做得越来越好，代价函数的值应该会随之下降。

接着，我们会从通用深度学习代价函数的角度，来看待这个更偏理论的方法。

最后，我们会从概率的视角来审视这个代价函数。

因为变分推断是贝叶斯机器学习的一部分，所以概率视角是特别相关的。

那么，现在揭晓答案。

我们要优化的目标函数叫做 ELBO，也就是证据下界。

它之所以代表“证据下界”，这个原因放到后面会清楚很多。

在 TensorFlow 里，由于优化器只有最小化功能，没有最大化功能，我们会去最小化这个值的负数。

所以，我们的代价是负的 ELBO，但我们真正试图去做的却是最大化 ELBO。

ELBO 由两项构成。它是数据的期望对数似然，减去给定 x 下 z 的分布 q 与 z 的分布 p 之间的 KL 散度。

我们会在接下来的几张幻灯片里解释这到底是什么意思。

首先，我们来谈谈期望对数似然。

这一项很棒的地方在于，它不过是一种非常花哨的说法，实际上做的依然是我们几乎一直在做的事情。

对数似然的期望值是什么？

尽管这看起来非常奇怪，但它实际上就是原始数据与重建数据之间的负交叉熵。

换句话说，假设输入和输出都服从伯努利分布，我们就可以把它计算为 x 乘以 x_hat 的对数，再加上一减 x 乘以一减 x_hat 的对数。

我们能这么做，是因为 x 本身就代表着概率。

此外，如果你假定输入和重建值服从高斯分布，那么你会用到平方误差再加上一些常数，因为平方误差正是一个高斯分布的对数似然。

所以事实上，这些重建损失，和我们之前那门无监督深度学习课里，用原始自编码器时所用的完全一样。

接下来，我们有 KL 散度这一项。

也就是给定 x 下 z 的分布 q 与 z 的分布 p 之间的 KL 散度。

在贝叶斯机器学习中，我们把 z 的分布 p 称为先验。

而在贝叶斯机器学习里，这个先验是由我们来选的。

为了方便起见，我们选择 N(零, 一)，即标准正态分布。

请注意，选择 N(零, 一) 并非基于什么理论基础。

我们选它仅仅是因为方便。

事实上，这有时也是贝叶斯机器学习的一个弱点。

如果你的先验选得不好，即便你的模型本身是合理的，也可能导致糟糕的结果。

那么，KL 散度起到什么作用呢？

我们之前讨论过这个，但怕你可能忘了，KL 散度是一种比较两个概率分布的方法。

如果两个概率分布一模一样，那么得到的值就是零。

如果两个概率分布有所不同，我们就会得到一个大于零的值。

两个概率分布差异越大，KL 散度的值就越大。

所以，这只是一个鼓励 z 的分布 q 趋向于标准正态分布的项。

记住，那正是我们想让隐向量呆的地方。

现在，我们来谈谈为什么 ELBO 以及构成它的这两项是合理的。

一种解读方式是，一个代价函数通常由这样两个部分组成。

第一部分告诉我们，我们的输出离目标有多近。

无论我们是在做回归、分类，还是在重建输入，情况都是这样。

第二部分则是一个正则化项。

这是许多机器学习模型中都很常见的结构，包括深度学习，你过去也见过，甚至在支持向量机里也有。

事实上，在你读到的许多论文里，它们直接就从这个前提出发了。

好了，这是让你自己确信这个代价函数是可靠的其中一种方式。

它由代价函数常见的两部分组成：一项表示我们离目标有多近，另一项表示一个正则化因子。

所以，我们到目前为止所做的，现在来说应该足够好了。

我们不知道代价函数为什么是这个形式，但我们知道它是有道理的。

我们后面会去看看代价函数为何采取这种形式，但那会需要多不少的理论和数学。

既然这些对实现代码来说并非必需，我们眼下就暂且略过这部分。

英文原文

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class, Unsupervised Deep Learning Part 2.

In this lecture, we are going to start talking about how to train an autoencoder.

Remember that with all machine learning problems, we typically have two modes of operation.

One is inference, and the other is training or learning.

Inference is a broad and probably overused term,but in the context of deep learning it usually refers to the forward direction, or in other words, making predictions in the context of supervised learning.

and transforming data into its latent representation in the context of unsupervised learning.

In scikit-learn, these are typically done by the functions fit and predict or fit and transform.

In any case, the next step is to discuss fitting or training.

As usual, what we would like to do is define a cost function and then minimize it.

So this is where things get a little tricky when we talk about variational autoencoders.

The cost function is going to look very strange and unfamiliar.

In fact, this is a common theme in this course.

This is the part where it would really benefit you to know about variational inference, but we will make it through by discussing things in terms of first principles, or in other words, stuff you already know by now.

So there is no background on variational inference required.

Let's first start out with what the cost function is.

Once we've seen that, then we'll ask, does it make sense?

Or in other words, does it decrease as the autoencoder improves at what it's supposed to do?

So that's the practical perspective.

As the autoencoder gets better at reconstructing its input, the cost function should decrease.

Then we'll look at a more theoretical approach in terms of general deep learning cost functions.

Finally, we'll look at the cost function from a probabilistic perspective.

Because variational inference is part of Bayesian machine learning, the probabilistic perspective is of particular relevance.

So now the big reveal.

The objective function we want to optimize is called the elbow, the evidence lower bound.

The reason this stands for evidence lower bound will make much more sense later.

In TensorFlow, since the optimizer only has a minimized function and not a maximized function, we'll be minimizing the negative of this.

So our cost is the negative elbow, but what we are really trying to do is maximize the elbow.

The elbow consists of two terms. It is the expected log likelihood of the data minus the KL divergence between Q of Z given X and P of Z.

We'll explain what this means over the next few slides.

First, let's talk about the expected log likelihood.

What's great about this is that it's just a very fancy way of doing what we are pretty much always doing.

What is the expected value of a log likelihood?

Although this looks really strange, it's actually just the negative cross entropy between the original data and the reconstructed data.

In other words, assuming the inputs and outputs are Bernoulli, we can calculate it as x log x hat plus one minus x log one minus x hat.

We can do this because the x themselves represent probabilities.

In addition, if you assumed your inputs and reconstructions are Gaussian, you would use squared error plus some constants because squared error is the log likelihood of a Gaussian.

So in fact, these are just the same reconstruction losses we used for our original autoencoder in the previous unsupervised deep learning course.

Next, we have the KL divergence term.

We have the KL divergence between Q of Z given X and P of Z.

In Bayesian machine learning, we call P of Z the prior.

And in Bayesian machine learning, it's up to us to choose it.

For convenience, we choose N zero one, the standard normal.

Please note that there is no theoretical underpinning for choosing N zero one.

We're just choosing it because it's convenient.

In fact, this is sometimes a weakness of Bayesian machine learning.

If you choose a poor prior, it could lead to bad results even though your model is sound.

So what does the KL divergence do?

We've discussed this before, but just in case you don't remember, the KL divergence is a way for us to compare two probability distributions.

If the two probability distributions are exactly the same, then we get zero.

If the two probability distributions are different, we get something greater than zero.

The more different the two probability distributions are, the bigger the KL divergence.

So this is just a term that encourages Q of Z to tend toward the standard normal distribution.

And remember, that's where we want our latent vectors to live.

Now let's talk about why the elbow and the two terms that make it up make sense.

One way to interpret this is that a cost function is usually made up of these two parts.

The first part tells us how close our output is to the target.

This is the case whether we're doing regression, classification, or reconstructing the input.

The second part is a regularizer.

This is a common construction in many machine learning models, including deep learning, as you've seen in the past, and even support vector machines.

In fact, in many papers you'll read, they simply start from this premise.

Okay, so that's one way to confirm to yourself that this cost function is sound.

It's made up of the usual two parts of a cost function, one term representing how close we are to the target, and one term representing a regularizer.

So what we've done so far should be good enough for now.

We don't know why the cost function is the way it is, but we know it makes sense.

We will look at why the cost function takes this form, but it will require much more theory and math.

Since it's not needed for implementation, we are just going to skip over it for now.

19 - TensorFlow实现 第1部分

大家好，欢迎回到《无监督深度学习》第二部分。

这节课，我们要讨论如何在 TensorFlow 中实现一个变分自编码器。

希望在听完这堂课后，你就能自己动手去尝试做这个实现。

当然，如果你遇到困难，随时可以去参考我提供的那份可以跑通的实现代码。

那么，我们先从我们想要达成的目标的一个基本轮廓开始。

变分自编码器，说到底依然只是一个神经网络，它有一个代价函数，而我们要用梯度下降来减小这个代价函数的值。

所以，至少我们要构建一个自编码器类，它拥有一个接收训练集的 fit 函数。

这个 fit 函数会对我们定义好的代价函数，也就是 ELBO，执行梯度下降，当然，最后我们也要画出代价曲线，来确保它收敛了。

这就是基本的框架。

那么，为了构建代价函数，我们需要定义怎样从输入到达重建值。

你已经看到怎样在编码器末端从输入得到高斯的参数了。

我们知道，下一步是从这个高斯分布中采样，然后把采样得到的样本喂给解码器，来拿到重建值。

把样本喂给解码器很简单，因为那不过就是把数据传入神经网络，这我们已经知道怎么做了。

这里的技巧在于如何取得这个样本，因为一旦你从一个分布里取了样本，在那之前的所有东西就未必是可微的了。

我们可以用一些数值技巧来让采样过程变得可微，这点我们稍后会看，但在 TensorFlow 里，我们需要用到一些特殊的库函数。

具体来说，你要留意的是 stochastic tensor 这个模块。

你可以在 tf.contrib.bayesflow.stochastic_tensor 这里找到它。

它的用法，就是你在这里看到的这行语法。

基本上，这几行代码说的是，我们想要一个值类型，它是一个样本，而这个样本将是一个随机张量，它来自一个具有这个均值和这个标准差的正态分布。

正态分布就是由这个均值和标准差定义的，而如何得到它们，我之前已经演示过了。

一旦你这样做了，并对 z 做了某些计算，最终得出代价，TensorFlow 就自动会知道该如何去调整那些用来计算均值和标准差的参数。

接下来是如何构建代价函数。

记住，它有两部分，期望对数似然，以及给定 x 下 z 的分布 q 与先验 z 的分布 p 之间的 KL 散度。

那么，我们先来谈谈期望对数似然。

如你所知，这就是二元交叉熵，或者更准确地说，是输入数据 x 和重建值 x_hat 之间的负二元交叉熵，我们把这两者都视作伯努利分布的均值。

幸运的是，TensorFlow 已经有一个你可以拿来用的二元交叉熵函数。

不过，由于数值稳定性问题，并不推荐你直接使用交叉熵定义的那个直白写法。

另一种选择是创建一个伯努利分布对象，然后计算数据在重建分布下的对数概率密度函数。

在代码里，我会给你展示如何同时使用这两种方式。

如果你不确定它们怎么会是等价的或为什么是这样，我建议你在纸上推演一下，这是个很好的练习。

你也可以写一些 numpy 代码来让自己确信它们是相等的。

但有件事你绝对不要做，那就是直接用那个定义公式，因为它数值上并不稳定。

接下来是 KL 散度这一项。

幸运的是，TensorFlow 自带一个叫 kl_divergence 的函数。

它接收两个分布对象作为参数，并输出它们之间的 KL 散度。

要注意，推导两个高斯分布之间的 KL 散度闭合解也是可能的，而我们在 Theano 即将采用的实现方式正是这个，因为 Theano 看起来没有 KL 散度的函数。

这也是我总说，用 TensorFlow 更像是在练习阅读文档，而不是在做机器学习的原因之一。

总之，一旦你有了 KL 散度和期望对数似然，就能计算出 ELBO，从那开始，创建一个优化器来做梯度下降，应该就是直截了当的事了。

我们要讨论的最后一件事是，我们到底想要我们的神经网络能计算哪些功能？

到目前为止，我们知道自己希望能从输入 x 得到重建值 x_hat。

回想一下，我们称这为后验预测样本。

但我们还想做别的什么呢？

以下这些功能并非全都绝对必要，但会给我们后面想做的一些实验带来很多便利。

其一是从先验 z 的分布 p 中抽取一个样本，也就是一个标准正态，然后从那里生成一个样本。

回想一下，我们称这为先验预测样本。

之后呢，我们想去映射出隐空间 z，看看每个部分都代表了什么数字。

为了做到这点，我们就需要有一个函数，能够接收一个给定的 z，请注意不是样本，然后生成一张图像。

为此，我们会用伯努利分布的均值，而不是去生成一个样本，当然你真要这么做也行。

好了，希望这些引导足以让你开始着手写代码。

总结一下，我们需要构建包含采样步骤的前向运算，为此我们得利用 stochastic tensor 这个模块。

从那开始，我们就能构建代价函数并对其进行优化。

我们还希望我们的自编码器具备一些标准操作，比如生成一个后验预测样本，从一个随机采样的 z 生成一个先验预测样本，以及从一个给定的 z 生成一个先验预测样本。

英文原文

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class, Unsupervised Deep Learning Part 2.

In this lecture, we are going to talk about how we're going to implement a variational autoencoder in TensorFlow.

Hopefully, after listening to this lecture, you'll be able to go ahead and attempt to do the implementation on your own.

Of course, if you have trouble, you can always refer to the working implementation I've provided.

So let's start out with a basic outline of what we want to do.

A variational autoencoder is at its heart, still just a neural network, and it has a cost function, and we want to reduce that cost function using gradient descent.

So at the very least, we are going to build an autoencoder class, which has a fit function that takes in a training set.

This fit function is going to perform gradient descent on the cost function we've defined, the elbow, and of course we are going to plot the cost at the end to ensure it converges.

So that's the basics.

Now in order to build the cost function, we need to define how to get from the input to the reconstruction.

You've already seen how to get the input to the Gaussian parameters at the end of the encoder.

We know that the next step is to sample from the Gaussian and then feed that sample to the decoder to get the reconstruction.

Feeding the sample to the decoder is simple, since that's just passing data into the neural network, which we already know how to do.

The trick here is how to take the sample, because once you take a sample from a distribution, nothing that came before it is necessarily differentiable.

There are some numerical tricks you can do to make the sample differentiable, which we'll look at later, but in TensorFlow, there are some special library functions we need.

Specifically, you want to pay attention to the stochastic tensor module.

This can be found at tfcontribbaseflow.stochastic tensor.

The way you use it is with this syntax that you see here.

Basically, what this is saying is we want a value type, which is a sample, and the sample is going to be a stochastic tensor that comes from this normal distribution with this mean and this standard deviation.

The normal distribution is defined by this mean and this standard deviation, which I showed you how to obtain earlier.

Once you do this, and you do some calculation on Z which eventually leads you to the cost, TensorFlow automatically knows how to adjust the parameters that went into calculating the mean and standard deviation.

Next is how to build the cost function.

Remember that there are two parts, the expected log likelihood and the KL divergence between Q of Z given X and the prior P of Z.

So let's first talk about the expected log likelihood.

As you know, this is binary cross entropy, or more accurately, the negative binary cross entropy between the input data x and the reconstruction x hat, which we are treating like Bernoulli means.

Luckily, TensorFlow already has a binary cross entropy function that you can use for this.

It is not recommended to use the straightforward definition of cross entropy due to numerical stability issues.

Another option is to create a Bernoulli distribution object and calculate the log PDF of the data under the reconstruction distribution.

In the code, I will show you how to use both options.

If you are not sure how or why these are the same, I would recommend writing it down on paper as it's a very good exercise.

You can also write some numpy code to convince yourself that they are the same.

But one thing you should definitely not do is use the direct definition since it is not numerically stable.

Next, there is the KL divergence.

Luckily, TensorFlow comes with a function called KL divergence.

It takes in two distribution objects as arguments and outputs the KL divergence.

Note that it is also possible to derive the KL divergence between two Gaussians in closed form, and this is what we'll be using in our Theano implementation since it doesn't seem to have a KL divergence function.

This is why I always say using TensorFlow is more like an exercise in reading documentation rather than an exercise in machine learning.

In any case, once you have the KL divergence and the expected log likelihood, you can calculate the elbow, and from there it should be straightforward to create an optimizer to do gradient descent.

The final thing we want to talk about is what functions do we actually want our neural network to compute?

So far, we know we want to be able to get a reconstruction x hat from the input x.

Recall that we call this the posterior predictive sample.

But what else do we want to do?

These are not all strictly necessary, but will be useful for some later experiments that we want to do.

The first is drawing a sample from the prior P of Z, which is a standard normal, and then generating a sample from that.

Recall that we call this the prior predictive sample.

Later, we'll want to map the latent space Z to see what digits each part represents.

In order to do that, we'll want to have a function that can take in a given Z, not a sample, and generate an image.

For this, we'll be using the Bernoulli means and not generating a sample, although you could.

Okay, so hopefully that's enough direction to get started on the code.

To summarize, we need to build the forward operation, which involves sampling, and for that we need to make use of the stochastic tensor module.

From there, we can build the cost function and optimize it.

We also want our autoencoder to have some standard operations, like generating a posterior predictive sample, generating a prior predictive sample from a randomly sampled z, and generating a prior predictive sample from a given z.

21 - TensorFlow实现 第3部分

大家好，欢迎回到《无监督深度学习》第二部分。

这节课，我会带你走一遍变分自编码器的 TensorFlow 实现。

如果你不想自己尝试写代码，尽管我非常建议你这么做了，对应的文件是 vae_tf.py。

在脚本最上面，我们有一系列导入。

util.py 只是一个帮我们加载数据的本地文件。

你想看也可以看看，但里面都是些很标准的东西，我估计你到现在闭着眼睛都能写出来了。

接着，就像你预料的那样，我们导入了 numpy、matplotlib 和 tensorflow。

然后，TensorFlow 里有些嵌套得非常深的模块，我们想给它们起一些更方便调用的名字。

再往下是 dense layer 类。

在构造器里，我们初始化了权重矩阵和偏置向量，并设置了激活函数。

在前向函数中，我们定义了前向运算，也就是常规的线性变换，然后让它通过激活函数。

接下来是变分自编码器类。

这个类里大部分的工作都将在构造器中完成，所以这个脚本里接下来要讲的就是最重要的那部分了。

那么，它接收参数 D，也就是输入维度，以及隐藏层大小的列表。

这个列表的最后一个元素，将是编码器输出的尺寸，同时也将是解码器输入的尺寸。

我们假定，解码器各层的尺寸，就是编码器尺寸的逆序。

接着，我们为数据创建一个占位符，叫做 x。

然后，我们创建编码器的各层，它们由 dense layer 对象组成，但只创建到倒数第二层。

这是因为，隐藏层会默认使用 relu 激活函数，但编码器的输出层我们想用点别的。

记住，编码器必须输出一个高斯分布的均值和标准差，所以正如我们所见，均值部分将不使用激活函数，而标准差部分会使用 softplus 激活函数。

不过，这些我们可以在层本身之外来定义。

这也解释了，为什么输出大小是二乘以 m。

接下来，我们拿到编码器的输出，将均值和标准差分离开，并对标准差施加 softplus。

注意，我们给标准差加了一个微小的平滑参数，这样我们就不会得到太接近于零的数，因为那会导致奇点问题。

然后，我们抽取一个样本 z，它是一个 stochastic tensor，这个你之前看过一个简单的例子了。

至此，编码器部分就全部讲完了。

现在来看解码器。

在这里，我们为每个隐藏层大小创建一个 dense layer，但顺序是反的，因为我们要让它的形状跟编码器对称。

当然，这意味着最末层的输出形状将是 D，也就是输入维度。

请注意，最末层的激活函数是空的，因为我们想把 logits 保留下来，TensorFlow 里有些函数接收的是 logits 而非实际概率。

这恰恰就是我们下一步要做的事情。

我们获取与这些 logits 对应的伯努利分布。

接下来，我们从该分布中抽取一个样本，我们之前聊过，这是我们要让自编码器具备的操作之一。

你可能也想看看输出的均值图像，那种情况下，你就需要施加 sigmoid。

再接着，我们想编写一些代码来抽取我们的先验预测样本。

因此，这第一步是从标准正态分布中抽取一个样本，其尺寸为 m，也就是隐向量的尺寸。

接着，我们把这个样本传过解码器，取出 logits，然后像之前一样创建一个伯努利分布。

到了这里，我们可以抽一个样本，但也可以仅对 logits 取 sigmoid 来得到输出均值。

这两种结果我们都会进行可视化。

最后，我们有一些代码来实现我们的第三个操作，即根据给定的 z 获取输出。

注意，这里我使用的是均值，但你也完全可以像我们之前那样去要一个样本。

接下来，我们来构建代价。

所以，记住它有两部分：KL 散度和期望对数似然。

正如你预期的那样，这就是我们之前定义的那个 z 与标准正态分布之间的 KL 散度。

请注意，我们是沿着轴一在做求和。

这是因为，像往常一样，我们的输出是一个 n 乘 D 的矩阵，所以我们希望得到每个样本的 KL 散度。

实际上，如果你对等价的多元高斯分布求 KL 散度，结果也是一样的，但由于每个维度是独立的，求和的做法是可行的。

接下来是期望对数似然，正如我们讨论过的，它等于负交叉熵。

在这下面，我注释掉了一些代码，那些代码实际在做负的二元交叉熵，两者是等价的。

请注意它用的是 logits。

最后，我们从期望对数似然里减去 KL 散度，就得到了 ELBO。

这里是针对每个样本求和。

但请注意，之前所有的操作都只是求和。

所以严格来说，早先在沿着轴一做求和这一步，其实并不是必须的，因为你做加法时，数字相加的顺序并不会影响最终总和。

最后，我们创建优化器、会话，并初始化所有变量。

在这个脚本里，把这些都放在模型内部来做很方便，这样可以确保 Theano 版本和 TensorFlow 版本拥有一模一样的 API。

接下来是 fit 函数。

幸运的是，大部分工作我们都已经做完了。

我们在这里要做的，仅仅是循环遍历所有 epoch，把数据分成 batch，然后为每个 batch 调用一次 train_op。

在最后，我们画出代价曲线，以此来验证一切都如预期那样在运转。

接着是 transform 函数，它会把输入 x 映射到对应的隐向量 z。

注意，我们在这里使用的是均值。

最后，我们有三个函数，分别对应我们早先定义的三种操作：一个用于从给定的 z 获取先验预测，一个用于从先验中采样的 z 来获取先验预测，还有一个用于获取后验预测样本。

再接下来是主函数，它会用到我们刚刚写的这些东西。

首先，我们获取数据，注意这里我们是把它转换成了真正的二值变量，也就是真正意义上的伯努利随机变量。

这一步并非绝对必要，但我之前了解到这样做效果会更好，所以你尽可以两种都试试。

接着，我们有一个循环，它会绘制从训练集中随机选取的数据点的重建结果。

脚本这么写，是为了能把原图和重建图并排显示，而你只需敲击字母 n 键，就可以退出这个循环。

然后，我们还有一个循环，用来生成先验预测样本。

请注意，它没有输入，因为 z 是直接从标准正态分布中采样的。

所以，这里也采用并排显示的方式，让我们能同时看到抽样本身，以及生成这个样本所依据的那个均值。

这同样是一个循环，你也可以按 n 键退出。

那么，我们把这段代码跑起来，看看结果如何。

Hey everyone, and welcome back to this class, Unsupervised Deep Learning Part 2.

In this lecture, I'm going to walk you through the TensorFlow implementation of a variational autoencoder.

If you don't want to try to code this yourself, although I highly recommend you do, the relevant file is vae tf.py.

So at the top, we have a bunch of imports.

util.py is just a local file that helps us load in the data.

You can examine it if you want, but it's standard stuff that I would assume you can do with your eyes closed by now.

Next, we import numpy, matplotlib, and tensorflow as one might expect.

And then there are a few modules in TensorFlow that are very deeply nested, so we want to give them more convenient names.

Next, we have the dense layer class.

In the constructor, we initialize the weight and the bias vector and set the activation function.

In the forward function, we define the forward operation, which is just the usual linear transformation and then passing it through the activation function.

Next, we have the variational autoencoder class.

Most of the work in this class will be in the constructor, so most of the important stuff in this script is coming up next.

So it takes in D, the input dimensionality, and the hidden layer sizes.

The final element of the hidden layer sizes list will be the size of the output of the encoder, which will also be the input size of the decoder.

We will assume that the sizes of the layers of the decoder will just be the sizes of the encoder in reverse.

Next, we create a placeholder for the data called x.

Then we create the layers of the encoder, which consists of dense layer objects, but only up to the second last layer.

This is because the hidden layers will use the rel u activation function by default, but will want to use something else for the output of the encoder.

Remember that the encoder has to output both the mean and standard deviation of a Gaussian, so the means will have no activation and the standard deviations will have a soft plus activation, as we've seen.

However, we can define those outside the layer itself.

This also explains why the output size is 2 times m.

Next, we get the output of the encoder, separate the means and standard deviations, and apply the soft plus.

Note that we've added a tiny smoothing parameter to the standard deviations so that we don't get a number too close to zero, which will cause a singularity.

Next, we draw a sample Z as a stochastic tensor, which you saw a simple example of earlier.

So that's everything for the encoder.

Now we have the decoder.

So here we create a dense layer for each of the hidden layer sizes, but we do it in reverse since we want it to be the opposite shape.

Of course, this means our final layer will have the output shape D, the input dimensionality.

Note that the activation function for the last layer is nothing, because we want to use the logits as TensorFlow will have functions that accept them rather than the actual probabilities.

And this is exactly what we do in the next step.

We get the Bernoulli distribution corresponding to these logits.

Next, we draw a sample from the distribution, which as we talked about, is one of the operations we wish for our autoencoder to have.

You may also want to see the mean output image, so in that case, you want to apply the sigmoid.

Next, we want to write some code to draw our prior predictive sample.

So the first step of this is to draw a sample from the standard normal of size m, the size of the latent vector.

Next, we pass this sample through the decoder, retrieve the logits, and then create a Bernoulli as we did before.

From here, we can draw a sample, but we can also just take the sigmoid of the logits to get the mean output.

We will be visualizing both.

Finally, we have some code for our third operation, getting the output for a given z.

Notice here that I'm using the mean, although you could also ask for a sample like we did before.

Next, we build the cost.

So remember that the two parts are the KL divergence and the expected log likelihood.

As you might expect, this is the KL divergence between Z and the standard normal we defined earlier.

Notice how we are summing along axis one.

This is because as usual, our output is an n by D matrix, so we want to have the KL divergence per sample.

This would actually be the same if you did a KL divergence on the equivalent multivariate Gaussian, but since each dimension is independent, summing the results is valid.

Next, we have the expected log likelihood, which as we discussed is the same as the negative cross entropy.

Below this, I have some code commented out that actually does the negative binary cross entropy, and this is equivalent.

Notice how it uses the logits instead.

Finally, we subtract the KL divergence from the expected log likelihood to get the elbow.

And here we sum across each sample.

But notice how everything was just summed.

So technically earlier, when we did the sum along axis one, it wasn't actually necessary, because when you add things, it doesn't matter what order you add them in, the result is the same.

Finally, we create our optimizer session and initialize all the variables.

It's convenient to do this inside the model in this script so that the theano version and the tensorflow version can have the exact same API.

Next, we have the fit function.

Luckily, we've already done most of the work.

All we need to do here is loop through the number of epochs, split the data into batches, and call a train op for each batch.

At the end, we plot the cost so that we can verify everything is working as it should.

Next, we have the transform function, which maps an input x into corresponding latent vector z.

Notice how we are using the means here.

Finally, we have three functions for the three operations we defined earlier: one for getting the prior predictive from a given z, one for getting the prior predictive from a z sampled from its prior, and one for getting the posterior predictive sample.

Next, we have the main function, which will make use of the stuff we just wrote.

First, we get the data, and notice how we are converting it to actual binary variables, which are proper Bernoulli random variables.

This isn't necessary, although I have read that it works better, so you can feel free to try both.

Next, we have a loop that will plot reconstructions of randomly selected data points from the training set.

It's written so that we plot the original and the reconstruction side by side, and you can just enter the letter N to exit the loop.

Next, we have a loop that will generate prior predictive samples.

Notice how this has no input because Z is just sampled from the standard normal.

So, this also does a side-by-side plot so that we can look at both the sample and the mean from which the sample was generated.

This is also a loop where you can hit N to exit.

So, let's run this and see what we get.

22 - 重参数化技巧

大家好，欢迎回到《无监督深度学习》第二部分。

这节课，我们要更详细地讲解变分自编码器中的一个组件。

跟往常一样，TensorFlow 很方便地帮我们把这个细节给隐藏了起来，让我们省了不少事，但与此同时，它看起来也像是变戏法一样。

所以这节课，我们就试着来弄明白这个戏法。

我们想讨论的这个特定组件，就是从给定 x 下 z 的分布 q 中采样的那个环节，它的输出是一个高斯分布。

请记住，这个采样是在我们把输入传过编码器之后发生的。

正如我们之前讨论过的，目前还不太清楚编码器里的权重是如何得以更新的，因为你没办法对包含采样操作的函数求梯度。

解决这个问题的方法，就是重参数化技巧。

我们之前在我的系列课程里，也就是那个 Python 中 NumPy 技术栈的第一门课里，多少见过这个。

我们当时做的一个练习，就是只用 numpy 从任意高斯分布中采样。

我们都知道，NumPy 里有 np.random.randn 这个函数。

它能给我们一个来自 N(零, 一) 的样本。

但当我们想从 N(mu, sigma 平方)，也就是一个带有任意均值和方差的高斯分布中采样时，该怎么办呢？

这个嘛，我们继续用 randn，但会把得到的结果乘以 sigma，再加上 mu。

如果你不信这样做能给出服从预期分布的样本，那我建议你动手在代码里生成一堆样本，然后算一算样本均值和方差。

你应该会发现，得到的结果会和预期一致。

一个去直观理解为什么这招管用的办法，是反过来想。

如你所知，在将数据用于机器学习算法之前，我们喜欢对它做标准化或归一化。

要做这个，我们会减去均值，再除以标准差。

这样就把一个具有任意均值和方差的变量，变成了一个均值为零、方差为壹的变量。

所以，如果 z 的均值为零、方差为壹，你就可以把这个等式重新整理成用 x 表示的式子，这样 x 当然就具有我们定义好的均值 mu 和方差 sigma 平方。

好，这些听起来可能都非常基础。

可它为什么又跟我们有关呢？

首先，我们来考虑一下，编码器输出的是 mu 和 sigma。

在这里，我明确地标出，mu 和 sigma 都是 x 和 theta 的函数，而 theta 是编码器的参数。

这代表的就是给定 x 下 z 的分布 q。

嗯，回想一下，在找到给定 x 下 z 的分布 q，也就是一个均值为 mu、标准差为 sigma 的高斯分布之后，我们需要从这个分布中抽一个样本。

那么，想想看，如果我们用刚才 numpy 那种获取样本的方法，会发生什么呢？

我们实际上会从 N(零,一) 中抓一个样本，乘以 sigma，再加上 mu。

那么，这为什么能帮到我们呢？

嗯，为了得到重建结果，我们会把 z 传过解码器。

但重要的是，在这种表述下，z 关于 theta 而言就不是随机的了，theta 代表的是编码器中我们需要去更新的那些变量。

换句话说，如果设我的目标函数为 J，那我就能轻松地计算出它关于 theta 的梯度。

这就是链式法则了。

dJ 比 d theta，等于 dJ 比 dz，乘以 dz 比 d theta。

然后，我可以把 z 替换成 mu 加上 sigma 乘以 epsilon。

再接着，我可以用加法规则，把 mu 和 sigma 关于 theta 分开求导。

而 epsilon 只是一个在前向传播过程中生成的数，所以关于 theta，它是常数。

这就是为什么我总说，用 Theano 能让你直接实现你所学到的东西，你总是可以只借助那些基本的构建模块，从零开始搭建一切。

而用 TensorFlow 呢，更像是在练习怎么查阅文档。

你得仔细搜遍文档，才能找出那个能达成你想要效果的东西，要不然就得找现成的代码，来当做实现目标的范例。

所以，有时候 Theano 是会啰嗦那么一点，但它在概念上反而会更容易理解。

英文原文

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class, Unsupervised Deep Learning Part 2.

In this lecture, we are going to go through one of the components in the variational autoencoder in more detail.

As usual, TensorFlow conveniently hides this detail from us so that we don't have as much work to do, but at the same time, it just looks like magic.

So in this lecture, we are going to try and understand this magic.

The particular component we want to talk about is the part where we sample from Q of Z given X, which outputs a Gaussian.

Remember, the sampling happens after we pass the input through the encoder.

As we discussed earlier, it's not entirely clear how the weights in the encoder can be updated because you can't take the gradient of a function that includes a sampling operation in it.

The solution to this is the reparameterization trick.

We sort of seen this before in the very first course in my series, the NumPy stack in Python.

One of the exercises we did was to sample from an arbitrary Gaussian using only numpy.

Now we know that NumPy has the function np.random.randn.

This gives us a sample from n zero one.

But what do we do when we want to sample from n of mu and sigma squared and arbitrary mean and variance?

Well we continue to use randn, but we multiply the result by sigma and add mu.

Now if you're not convinced that this gives you a sample from the desired distribution, I would challenge you to generate a bunch of samples in code and then calculate the sample mean and variance.

You should find that they come out to be as expected.

An intuitive way to think about why this works is to consider the opposite operation.

As you know, we like to standardize or normalize our data before using machine learning algorithms on it.

In order to do this, we subtract the mean and divide by the standard deviation.

That turns it from a variable with arbitrary mean and variance to a variable with mean zero and variance one.

So if z has mean zero and variance one, you can rearrange this equation to be in terms of x, and of course x has mean mu and variance sigma squared as we defined it to be.

Okay, so this all probably seems very basic.

Why is this relevant?

First, let's consider that the encoder outputs mu and sigma.

Here I am explicitly showing that mu and sigma are functions of x and theta, the parameters of the encoder.

This represents q of z given x.

Well, remember that after we find q of z given x, which is a Gaussian with mean mu and standard deviation sigma, we need to take a sample from this distribution.

Well, consider what would happen if we used our numpy method of obtaining this sample.

We would actually grab a sample from n01, multiply by sigma, and add mu.

So, why does this help us?

Well, to get the reconstruction, we are going to pass z through the decoder.

But what's important is that with this formulation, z is not stochastic with respect to theta, which represents the variables in the encoder that we need to update.

In other words, if I let my objective be j, then I can easily calculate the gradient with respect to theta.

This is just the chain rule.

DJ by d theta is dj by dz times dz by d theta.

Then I can replace z with mu plus sigma times epsilon.

Then I can differentiate mu and sigma with respect to theta separately using the addition rule.

And epsilon is just a number that was generated during the forward propagation, so with respect to theta, it is constant.

So this is why I always say using theano lets you directly implement what you've learned, and you can always build stuff from the ground up using just the basic building blocks.

Using TensorFlow is more like an exercise in reading documentation.

You need to scour the documentation to find the thing that does the thing that you want, or you have to find existing code that can act as an example for what you want to accomplish.

So sometimes Theano is a little bit more verbose, but it actually ends up being conceptually easier.

23 - Theano实现

大家好，欢迎回到《无监督深度学习》第二部分。

这节课，我们要在 Theano 中实现一个变分自编码器。

如果你不想自己尝试写代码，尽管我非常建议你这么做了，课程仓库里对应的文件是 vae_theano.py。

在脚本最上面，是我们常见的那些导入。

util.py 只是一个帮我们加载数据的本地文件。

你想看也可以看看，但里面都是些很标准的东西，我估计你到现在闭着眼睛都能写出来了。

接着，就像你预期的那样，我们导入了 numpy、matplotlib 和 theano。

请注意，我们还需要 theano 中的 RandomStreams 对象，因为在构建 theano 图时，就是用它来表征图中的随机数的。

再往下是 dense layer 类。

在构造器里，我们初始化了权重矩阵和偏置向量，并设置了激活函数。

在前向函数中，我们定义了前向运算，也就是常规的线性变换，然后让它通过激活函数。

接下来是变分自编码器类。

这个类里大部分的工作都将在构造器中完成，所以这个脚本里接下来要讲的就是最重要的那部分了。

那么，它接收参数 D，也就是输入维度，以及隐藏层大小的列表。

这个列表的最后一个元素，将是编码器输出的尺寸，同时也将是解码器输入的尺寸。

我们假定，解码器各层的尺寸，就是编码器尺寸的逆序。

接着，我们为数据创建一个占位符，叫做 x。

它是二维的，所以会是一个矩阵。

然后，我们创建编码器的各层，它们由 dense layer 对象组成，但只创建到倒数第二层。

这是因为，隐藏层会默认使用 relu 激活函数，但编码器的输出层我们想用点别的。

记住，编码器必须输出一个高斯分布的均值和标准差，所以正如我们所见，均值部分将不使用激活函数，而标准差部分会使用 softplus 激活函数。

不过，这些我们可以在层本身之外来定义。

这也解释了，为什么输出大小是二乘以 m。

接下来，我们拿到编码器的输出，将均值和标准差分离开，并对标准差施加 softplus。

注意，我们给标准差加了一个微小的平滑参数，这样我们就不会得到太接近于零的数，因为那会导致奇点问题。

然后，我们用之前见过的重参数化技巧来抽取样本 z，正是这个技巧让编码器的参数变得可微。

至此，编码器部分就全部讲完了。

现在来看解码器。

在这里，我们为每个隐藏层大小创建一个 dense layer，但顺序是反的，因为我们要让它的形状跟编码器对称。

当然，这意味着最末层的输出形状将是 D，也就是输入维度。

请注意，最末层的激活函数是 sigmoid，因为我们需要从输出中得到伯努利概率。

在这里，我们没法像在 TensorFlow 里那样去直接使用 logits 值。

接下来，我们将 z 实际传过解码器，并在末端拿到后验预测概率。

然后，我们从这个分布中采样，我们之前聊过，这是我们要让自编码器具备的操作之一。

再接着，我们想编写一些代码来抽取我们的先验预测样本。

因此，这第一步是从标准正态分布中抽取一个样本，其尺寸为 m，也就是隐向量的尺寸。

接着，我们像之前一样，把这个样本传过解码器，并取回输出。

到了这里，我们可以抽一个样本，但也可以直接绘制输出的均值。

这两种结果我们都会进行可视化。

最后，我们有一些代码来实现我们的第三个操作，即根据给定的 z 获取输出。

所以，我们需要定义一个占位符 z，它是二维的，也就是一个矩阵。

然后，我们将这个输入 z 传进解码器，得到输出的均值。

你也可以选择去取一个样本，不过我这里就不这么做了。

接下来，我们来构建代价。

那么，请记住它有两部分：KL 散度和期望对数似然。

正如你预期的那样，这就是我们之前定义的那个 z 与标准正态分布之间的 KL 散度。

如果你不确定如何求两个高斯分布之间的 KL 散度，注释里的这些链接应该能帮到你。

请注意，我们是沿着轴一在做求和。

这是因为，像往常一样，我们的输出是一个 n 乘 D 的矩阵，所以我们希望得到的是每个样本的 KL 散度。

实际上，如果你对等价的多元高斯分布求 KL 散度，结果也是一样的，但由于每个维度是独立的，求和的做法是可行的。

接下来是期望对数似然，正如我们讨论过的，它等于负交叉熵。

最后，我们从期望对数似然里减去 KL 散度，就得到了 ELBO，然后再对每个样本求和。

但请注意，我们之前所做的所有操作都只是求和。

所以严格来说，早先在沿着轴一做求和这一步，其实并不是必须的，因为你做加法时，数字相加的顺序并不会影响最终总和。

接下来，我们得定义如何执行更新操作。

为了让我们的 Theano 脚本和 TensorFlow 脚本保持一致，我们在这里也会使用 RMSProp。

所以，第一步是收集每一层的所有参数，然后求代价函数对这些参数的梯度。接着，定义一些 RMSProp 特有的变量，这些变量与 TensorFlow 中的默认值相匹配。

也就是，衰减率设为零点九。

再接着，我们定义缓存变量和新的缓存变量，因为它会包含更新后的值，然后就是定义实际的更新元组。

我们缩小一点画面，让你能看清楚。

最后，我们想用刚刚构建好的这些内容，来创建一些可调用的 Theano 函数。

请注意，在 Theano 里这么做要更方便一点。

我们不需要再额外定义实例方法，可以直接用 Theano 函数来赋值。

所以，首先我们有 train_op，它返回 ELBO 并执行更新操作。

接着，我们有一个用来获取后验预测样本的函数。

然后，我们有一个用来获取先验预测样本的函数。

再来是一个 transform 函数，给定一批 x，它返回编码器端高斯的均值。

再然后，我们有一个从给定 z 获取先验预测样本的函数。

接下来是 fit 函数。

幸运的是，大部分工作我们都已经做完了。

我们在这里要做的，仅仅是循环遍历所有 epoch，把数据分成 batch，然后为每个 batch 调用一次 train_op。

在最后，我们画出代价曲线，以此来验证一切都如预期那样在运转。

再接下来是主函数，它会用到我们刚刚写的这些东西。

首先，我们获取数据。

请注意，我们把它转换成了真正的二值变量，也就是真正意义上的伯努利随机变量。

这一步并非绝对必要，但我之前了解到这样做效果会更好。

你尽可以两种都试试。

接着，我们有一个循环，它会绘制从训练集中随机选取的数据点的重建结果。

脚本这么写，是为了能把原图和重建图并排显示，而你只需敲击字母 n 键，就可以退出这个循环。

然后，我们还有一个循环，用来生成先验预测样本。

请注意，它没有输入，因为 z 是直接从标准正态分布中采样的。

所以，这里也采用并排显示的方式，让我们能同时看到抽样本身，以及生成这个样本所依据的那个均值。

这同样是一个循环，你也可以按 n 键退出。

那么，我们把这段代码跑起来，看看结果如何。

英文原文

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class, Unsupervised Deep Learning Part 2.

In this lecture, we are going to implement a variational autoencoder in Theano.

If you don't want to try to code this yourself, although I strongly recommend you do, the relevant file in the course repo is vaethano.py.

So at the top we have our usual imports.

util.py is just a local file that helps us load in the data.

You can examine it if you want, but it's standard stuff that I would assume you can do with your eyes closed by now.

Next we have import numpy, matplotlib, and theano as one might expect.

Note that we also need the random streams object from theano since that's what is used for representing random numbers within the theano graph when we build it.

Next we have the dense layer class.

In the constructor we initialize the weight and bias vector and set the activation function.

In the forward function we define the forward operation which is just the usual linear transformation and then passing it through the activation function.

Next we have the variational autoencoder class.

Most of the work in this class will be in the constructor, so most of the important stuff in this script is coming up next.

So it takes in D, the input dimensionality, and the hidden layer sizes.

The final element of the hidden layer sizes list will be the size of the output of the encoder, which will also be the input size of the decoder.

We will assume that the sizes of the layers of the decoder will just be the sizes of the encoder in reverse.

Next we create a placeholder for the data called x.

It's 2D, so it's going to be a matrix.

Then we create the layers of the encoder, which consists of dense layer objects, but only up to the second last layer.

This is because the hidden layers will use the rel u activation function by default, but will want to use something else for the output of the encoder.

Remember that the encoder has to output both the mean and standard deviation of a Gaussian, so the means will have no activation and the standard deviations will have a soft plus activation as we've seen.

However, we can define those outside the layer itself.

This also explains why the output is size 2 times m.

Next, we get the output of the encoder, separate the means and standard deviations, and apply the soft plus.

Note that we've added a tiny smoothing parameter to the standard deviations so that we don't get a number too close to zero, which will cause a singularity.

Next, we draw a sample z using the reparameterization trick which you saw earlier, and that's what allows the encoder params to be differentiable.

So that's everything for the encoder.

Now we have the decoder.

So here we create a dense layer for each of the hidden layer sizes, but we do it in reverse since we want it to be the opposite shape.

Of course, this means our final layer will have output shape D, the input dimensionality.

Note that the activation function for the last layer is sigmoid because we want Bernoulli probabilities from the output.

There's nothing we can do with the logits like we can in TensorFlow.

Next, we actually pass Z through the decoder and retrieve the posterior predictive probabilities at the end.

Next, we sample from this distribution, which as we talked about is one of the operations we wish for our autoencoder to have.

Next, we want to write some code to draw our prior predictive sample.

Next, we pass this sample through the decoder and retrieve the output as we did before.

From here we can draw a sample, but we can also just plot the mean output.

We will be visualizing both.

Finally, we have some code for our third operation, getting the output for a given z.

So we need to define a placeholder z, which is 2D, so it's a matrix.

Then we pass this Z input into the decoder and get the output means.

You could also take a sample, but I'm not going to.

Next, we build the cost.

So remember that the two parts are the KL divergence and the expected log likelihood.

As you might expect, this is the KL divergence between Z and the standard normal we defined earlier.

If you are not sure how to find the KL divergence between two Gaussians, these links in the comments should help.

Notice how we are summing along axis one.

This is because as usual, our output is an n by D matrix, so we want the KL divergence per sample.

This would actually be the same if you did a KL divergence on the equivalent multivariate Gaussians, but since each dimension is independent, summing the results is valid.

Next, we have the expected log likelihood, which as we discussed is the same as the negative cross entropy.

Finally, we subtract the KL divergence from the expected log likelihood to get the elbow, and then we sum across each sample.

But notice how everything we did was just summed.

So technically earlier, when we did the sum along axis one, it wasn't actually necessary, because when you add things, it doesn't matter what order you add them in, the result is the same.

Next, we have to define how to do our updates.

In order for our Theano and TensorFlow script to be the same, we're going to be doing RMSProp here as well.

So let's scroll out here so you can see it.

Notice how this is a little more convenient in Theano.

We don't need to define extra instance methods, we can just assign them from the Theano functions directly.

Next we have a function to get the posterior predictive sample.

Then we have a function to get the prior predictive sample.

And then we have a function to get a prior predictive sample from a given z.

Luckily we've already done most of the work.

All we need to do here is loop through the number of epochs, split the data into batches, and call the train op for each batch.

At the end we plot the cost so we can verify everything is working as it should.

Next we have the main function which will make use of the stuff we just wrote.

Notice how this has no input because Z is just sampled from the standard normal.

24 - 隐空间可视化

大家好，欢迎回到《无监督深度学习》第二部分。

这节课，我们要做一个练习，来可视化一下隐空间。

具体来说，我们想做的是把隐空间的维度设为二。

这能让我们创建一个以零为中心的二维网格。

然后，利用这个由 z 值构成的网格，我们就能看到解码器会生成怎样的图像，而这些图像，正是隐空间中对应区域究竟学会了编码什么的视觉呈现。

那么，如果你想在 GitHub 上查看这段代码，课程仓库里对应的文件是 visualize_latent_space.py。

也请注意，你还能看到中心区域是模糊的，因为那里就好像是所有数字的交汇点。

25 - 贝叶斯视角

大家好，欢迎回到《无监督深度学习》第二部分。

那么，现在到了变分自编码器中比较难的部分了。

我们要来讨论一下，我们的代价函数是从哪里来的。

如果你不喜欢推演等式，并且完全不介意不懂背后的理论，那就请随意跳过接下来这部分内容。

对我们大多数人来说，那样的抽象程度已经足够了。

你给一个代价函数，我用梯度下降去最小化它，这样就行了。

但如果你对这个代价函数的来由，以及它跟变分推断的关联感兴趣，那我们就接着往下讲。

那么，我们想从给定 x 下 z 的分布 p 开始讲起。

我们不知道给定 x 下 z 的分布 p 是什么，但我们很想知道。

事实上，这正是我们自编码器在做的事情。

它用一个高斯分布，即给定 x 下 z 的分布 q，来近似给定 x 下 z 的分布 p。

这时候你可能会有一个疑问：我们为什么想要知道给定 x 下 z 的分布 p 呢？

我们称给定 x 下 z 的分布 p 为后验，总的来说，我们的目标是找到一个从 x 到 z 的良好映射。

为了把这个概念跟你已经知道的东西联系起来，回想一下高斯混合模型或隐马尔可夫模型。

在高斯混合模型里，给定 x 下 z 的分布 p 告诉我们 x 属于哪个簇，因为在 GMM 中，隐变量 z 对应的就是簇的身份。

而在聚类问题里，那显然就是我们想要达成的目标。

给我们一个数据点，我们就想知道它属于哪个簇。

在 HMM 中，z 指代的是隐藏状态。

我们也想找出这些状态，比如，假设 x 代表句子中的单词，而 z 代表其词性。

那么，如果我们想做词性标注，就必须找到 z。

另一种思考方式是从分类的角度来看。

在分类问题中，我们有我们的目标，姑且称之为给定 x 下 y 的分布 p，同时我们有神经网络的预测，姑且称之为给定 x 下 y 的分布 q。

通常，给定 x 下 y 的分布 p 在某个特定类别上是壹，在其他类别上则是零，但这依然是一个有效的概率分布，因此称其为给定 x 下 y 的分布 p 是完全成立的。

并且如你所知，对于分类问题，正确的代价函数是给定 x 下 y 的分布 p 与给定 x 下 y 的分布 q 之间的交叉熵。

而请记住，交叉熵就等于 KL 散度加上一个常数。

分类，或者说广义上的监督学习，与无监督学习之间唯一的区别就在于，在监督学习中，y 是给定的。

而 z 却并非已知。

它有点像一个未被观测到的目标。

那么至此，希望你能理解，为什么我们会想要去找到给定 x 下 z 的分布 p 了。

它就是无监督学习中，对应于寻找给定 x 下 y 的分布 p 这一任务的对等物。

所以，为了巩固这个概念：在监督学习中，我们想要找到给定 x 下 y 的分布 p，也就是真实的目标；而在无监督学习中，我们想准确找到给定 x 下 z 的分布 p，也就是真实的、未被观测到的变量。

现在，既然我们知道了想要让自己的近似，也就是给定 x 下 z 的分布 q，去接近给定 x 下 z 的分布 p，那我们要怎样才能做到这点呢？

这个嘛，我们之前已经讨论过了。

我们知道如何衡量两个概率分布之间的接近程度，那就是 KL 散度。

那么，我们来写下这两个分布之间 KL 散度的表达式。

现在我们可以看到，这是某个函数在某个概率分布上的积分。

这也正是期望值的定义，那我们就用期望值的形式来表示吧。

然后，我们当然可以用对数运算法则，把商拆分成减法。

下一步是对给定 x 下 z 的分布 p 应用贝叶斯规则。

记住，这等于给定 z 下 x 的分布 p 乘以 z 的分布 p，再除以 x 的分布 p。

于是我们从这里开始，这是上一张幻灯片上的等式，然后我们代入用贝叶斯规则得到的等价表达式。

接着，我们再次使用对数运算法则，把这些项拆分成相加的形式。

现在，由于期望是关于 z 来求的，而 x 的分布 p 并不依赖于 z，我们可以把它提到期望值的外面来。

下一步就只是一些代数运算了。

把 log p(x) 移到左边，并把各项的符号反转。

现在有意思的是，我们可以重新整理右边的这几项。

具体来说，让 log p(x|z) 自己单独待着，而把给定 x 下 z 的分布 q 和 z 的分布 p 放在一起。

我们可以看到，这实际上又是另一个 KL 散度。

具体来说，它是编码器分布，即给定 x 下 z 的分布 q，与我们关于 z 的先验，也就是 z 的分布 p，之间的 KL 散度。

如果你还记得的话，我们把这个先验设定为均值为零的单位高斯分布。

那么，这有意思在哪里呢？

如果你现在已经忘了，那也是情有可原的，因为我们刚刚看了一大堆数学推导，但等式右边，就等于我们早先定义的那个 ELBO。

而且既然左边等于右边，那这两者都是 ELBO 的有效表达式。

当然，在写代码时，我们会用右边那个表达式，因为它由我们实际能够计算的项组成。

而左边的表达式，我们实际上是任何一项都无法计算的。

然而，左边的表达式却为我们使用 ELBO 作为目标函数，提供了重要的理论依据。

我们用简单的形式把它写出来，这样看起来更清楚。

并且我们可以把这个式子重新整理，让 KL 散度项移到右边。

这里要意识到的重要一点是，左边的 log p(x) 关于 q 来说是固定不变的。

换言之，如果我改变了 ELBO，那么 KL 散度就会以某种方式随之改变，使左边那一项保持不变。

换句话说，通过最大化 ELBO，我就能最小化 q 与真实后验之间的 KL 散度，而这，正如我们先前所说的那样，正是我们最初的目标。

这也解释了为什么我们的代价函数叫做 ELBO。

回想一下，x 的分布 p 被称为证据。

现在我们看到，证据由 ELBO 和 KL 散度这两部分构成。

由于任何 KL 散度的值都必须大于或等于零，那么 log p(x) 就必须总是大于或等于 ELBO。

而又因为左边代表证据，ELBO 就代表了证据的一个下界，由此便得了“证据下界”这个名字。

这节课内容很长，我们现在来做个总结。

起初，我只是直接给了你一个目标函数，并说我们打算去最小化它。

我们从实际的角度审视了它，以确认它是合乎情理的。

这个目标函数由两部分组成：重建误差和正则化项。

我们称其为 ELBO。

我们也把这个和深度学习的理念以及更广义的机器学习联系了起来。

我们指出，在不少情况下，研究的出发点往往就是“目标等于目标输出误差加上一个正则化项”这样一种组合，所以，从这个意义上讲，我们所做的事情是完全讲得通的。

接着，我们从概率的视角审视了这个目标函数，并注意到我们真正想做的，是去近似那个后验分布——给定 x 下 z 的分布 p。

然后，我们通过一些代数运算和概率规则的运用，证明了最大化 ELBO，就等价于最小化我们的近似分布 q 与真实分布（给定 x 下 z 的分布 p）之间的 KL 散度。

最后，我想为那些以前学过变分推断的同学做一个小小的注解。

有一种用于解决变分推断问题的技术，叫做平均场近似。

这个我就不展开解释了，因为如果你尚不知道它是什么的话，那这个注解也就没有太大关系。

但你或许跟我一样，之前也一直在变分自编码器里寻找某种类似平均场近似的东西。

那么，这里只想确认一下：变分自编码器的解并没有用到平均场近似，因此，它也不在我们的讨论范围之内。

英文原文

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class, Unsupervised Deep Learning, Part 2.

So now comes the hard part of the variational autoencoder.

We're going to discuss where our cost function comes from.

If you don't like manipulating equations and you're completely comfortable not knowing the theory, then feel free to skip ahead.

For most of us, that's a good enough abstraction.

You give me a cost function, I use gradient descent to minimize it, and that's enough.

But if you're interested in where this cost function comes from and how it's related to variational inference, then let's continue.

So we want to start with P of Z given X.

We don't know what P of Z given X is, but we would like to.

In fact, that's exactly what our autoencoder does.

It approximates P of Z given X with a Gaussian Q of Z given X.

Now one question you might have is why do we want to know P of Z given X?

We call P of Z given X the posterior, and generally speaking, our goal is to find a good mapping from X to Z.

To relate this concept to something you already know about, think of Gaussian mixture models or hidden Markov models.

With Gaussian mixture models, P of Z given X tells us which cluster X belongs to, because Z, the latent variable, corresponds to the cluster identity in GMMs.

And with clustering, clearly that is our intended goal.

We are given a data point and we want to know which cluster it belongs to.

With HMMs, Z refers to the hidden state.

We want to know those, for instance, if X represents the words in a sentence and Z represents the parts of speech.

So if we want to do parts of speech tagging, we have to find Z.

Another way to think of this is in terms of classification.

With classification, we have our targets, let's call them P of Y given X, and we have our neural network predictions, let's call them Q of Y given X.

Now typically, P of Y given X is just one for one particular class and zero elsewhere, but that's still a valid probability distribution, therefore calling it P of Y given X is completely valid.

and as you know, for classification, the correct cos function is the cross entropy between P of y given x and q of y given x.

And remember the cross entropy is the same as the K L divergence plus a constant.

The only difference between classification or really just supervised learning in general and unsupervised learning is that y is given in supervised learning.

Z is not given.

It's kind of like an unobserved target.

So hopefully it makes sense why we want to find P of Z given X.

It is the unsupervised equivalent of finding P of Y given X.

So just to solidify this concept, in supervised learning, we want to find P of Y given X, the true targets, and in unsupervised learning, we want to accurately find P of Z given X, the true unobserved variables.

Now that we know we want to make our approximation Q of Z given X close to P of Z given X, how can we do that?

Well, we have already discussed this.

We know how to measure the closeness between two probability distributions, that is the KL divergence.

So let's write down the expression for the KL divergence between these two distributions.

Now we can see that this is an integral of some function over a probability distribution.

This is also the definition of expected value, so let's use the expected value instead.

And of course we can use the log rule to split up the quotient into a subtraction.

The next step is to apply Bayes' rule to p of z given x.

Remember that this is p of x given z times p of z divided by p of x.

So we start with this, which is the equation from the last slide, and then we plug in the equivalent expression using Bayes' rule.

And again, we can use the log rule to split these up into additive terms.

Now since the expectation is with respect to z and p of x does not depend on z, we can bring that outside the expected value.

The next step is just some algebra.

Bring log p of x to the left side and invert the signs.

Now what's interesting is we can rearrange the terms on the right side.

In particular, let's leave log p of x given z by itself, but put q of z given x and p of z together.

What we can see is that this is actually another KL divergence.

In particular it's the KL divergence between the encoder distribution, q of z given x and our prior on z p of z.

If you recall we are setting this prior to be the unit Gaussian with mean zero.

So what is interesting about this?

Well you can be forgiven if you've forgotten by now because we've just looked at a lot of math, but the right side is equal to the elbow we defined earlier.

And since the left side is equal to the right side, they are both valid expressions for the elbow.

Of course in code we are going to use the right side because it consists of things that we can actually calculate.

Whereas on the left side we can't actually calculate any of it.

However, the left side gives us important justification for using the elbow as our objective.

Let's write it out in simple terms so it's easier to look at.

And we can rearrange this so that the KL divergence is on the right side.

The important thing to realize here is that the left side p of x is fixed with respect to q.

So in other words, if I change the elbow, then the kale divergence will change in such a way that keeps the left side constant.

In other words, by maximizing the elbow, I minimize the KL divergence between Q and the true posterior, which is, as we stated earlier, our original goal.

This also explains why our cost function is called the elbow.

Recall that p of x is called the evidence.

Now we can see that the evidence is made up of the elbow and the KL divergence.

Since any KL divergence must be greater than or equal to zero, log p of x must always be greater than or equal to the elbow.

And since the left side represents the evidence, the elbow represents a lower bound for the evidence, hence the name evidence lower bound.

Let's now summarize this lecture since it was quite long.

Originally, I just gave you an objective function and said that we're going to minimize it.

We looked at it from a practical standpoint to confirm that it made sense.

The objective is made up of two parts, the reconstruction error and the regularization term.

We call this the elbow.

We also connected this back to deep learning concepts and machine learning in general.

We noted that in some instances, it is often the starting premise that the objective be a combination of the target output error plus a regularization term.

so in that sense what we are doing makes total sense.

Next we looked at the objective from a probabilistic perspective, noting that what we really want to do is approximate the posterior P of Z given X.

We then showed with some algebra and applying the rules of probability that maximizing the elbow is equivalent to minimizing the KL divergence between our approximation Q and the true P of Z given X.

Lastly, I want to make a small note for those of you who have learned about variational inference before.

One technique used to solve variational inference problems is called the mean field approximation.

I'm not going to explain what this is, since this note is irrelevant if you don't already know what it is.

But like me, you may have been looking for something resembling the mean field approximation in the variational autoencoder.

So just to confirm, the variational autoencoder solution does not use the mean field approximation, and hence it won't be part of our discussion.

26 - 变分自编码器章节总结

大家好，欢迎回到《无监督深度学习》第二部分。

这节课，我们对这一部分所涵盖的内容做个总结。

这部分讲的都是变分自编码器，它结合了深度学习和贝叶斯机器学习，特别是变分推断中的技术。

变分自编码器学会了两件事。

第一，它能重建自己的输入。

第二，也是最重要的，它包含一个瓶颈，这意味着自编码器必须学习一个紧凑且高效的输入数据表征。

我们看到了变分自编码器与普通自编码器之间的一个主要区别：由于变分自编码器是贝叶斯式的，我们在感兴趣的每一层所表征的都是一个分布。

所以，在编码器末端，我们有一个高斯分布；而在输入和输出端，我们有的是伯努利分布。

我们看到了，前向运算的执行方式跟我们习以为常的做法有点不同。

当我们到达编码器末端时，我们从这个高斯分布中抽取一个样本，然后将这个样本传过解码器。

在 TensorFlow 中，这个操作是通过 stochastic tensor 模块来完成的，而在 Theano 里，这个操作是通过重参数化技巧来完成的。

我个人更喜欢重参数化技巧，因为它的通用性更强。

接着，我们审视了变分自编码器的代价函数。

我们从查看代价函数开始，并确保它是合理的。

我们发现，它由两项组合而成：期望对数似然和 KL 散度。

我们看到，期望对数似然跟负交叉熵是一回事，因此这一部分与我们在普通自编码器上所用的代价函数也是相同的。

所以，这很合理，因为许多机器学习模型的代价函数，都是由这两项组合而成的。一项负责惩罚输出与目标之间的误差，另一项负责正则化。

再接下来，我们从概率的视角审视了代价函数。

我们始于这样一个前提：我们想要做的事是，精确地近似后验分布——给定 x 下 z 的分布 p。

这是合理的，因为在监督学习中，我们做的其实是一样的事，只不过我们管输出叫 y，且给定了给定 x 下 y 的分布 p，而我们在尝试做的则是找到一个好的给定 x 下 y 的分布 q，来精确逼近给定 x 下 y 的分布 p。

无监督学习的不同点在于，给定 x 下 z 的分布 p 是未知的，然而我们仍想找到给定 x 下 z 的分布 q 来精确地近似它。

为了达成这个目标，我们进行了一些巧妙的数学推导，为我们所称的 ELBO 找到了几个等价的表达式。

这两个 ELBO 的表达式，以不同的方式帮助了我们。

其一是，它就是我们在早先见过的那个代价函数，我们能在代码中把它算出来，并像优化其他任何代价函数一样去优化它。

另一种表达则向我们揭示了为什么它被称为 ELBO。

它代表的是证据下界，并且随着这个值的增加，给定 x 下 z 的分布 p 与给定 x 下 z 的分布 q 之间的 KL 散度会减小，而这，正是我们最初的目标。

英文原文

Hey everyone and welcome back to this class, Unsupervised Deep Learning, Part 2.

In this lecture, we are going to summarize what we've covered in this section.

This section was all about variational autoencoders, which combine techniques from both deep learning and Bayesian machine learning, specifically variational inference.

Variational autoencoders learn how to do two things.

Firstly, it can reconstruct its input.

Secondly, and most importantly, it contains a bottleneck, which means the autoencoder has to learn a compact and efficient representation of the input data.

We saw that one big difference between variational autoencoders and regular autoencoders is that since variational autoencoders are Bayesian, what we are representing at each layer of interest is a distribution.

So at the end of the encoder, we have a Gaussian distribution, and at the input and output, we have Bernoulli distributions.

We saw that the way that we perform the forward operation is a little bit different from what we're used to.

When we get to the end of the encoder, we draw a sample from this Gaussian and then pass this sample through the decoder.

In TensorFlow, this operation is done using the stochastic tensor module, and in Theano, this operation is done using the reparameterization trick.

I personally like the reparameterization trick better because it's more generally applicable.

Next, we looked at the cost function for the variational autoencoder.

We started out just by looking at the cost function and making sure it made sense.

We saw that it was the combination of two terms, the expected log likelihood and the KL divergence.
